# crust — full audit (docs + website + code + probes)

Started 2026-10-17. Baseline repos: crust `c34ca65`, website `b314237`, KB
`e4e00057` (all `main`, clean; `agents` submodule pointer still at `90731faf`,
bump deferred to G12 by design).

Structure:
- (a) Doc pages read + claims extracted
- (b) Probes run (exact command + output + exit code)
- (c) Findings (each with evidence)
- (d) Fixed in place (trivial fixes, with commit SHAs)
- (e) G13+ created

Status legend for claims: `V` verified by probe, `C` confirmed in code (no probe needed),
`X` claim contradicted (finding opened), `?` unresolved.

The mirror shape (node declares `properties` once, each branch says which it needs — urlbox.io's `RenderRequest`) now composes (was `{}` → 0 cases). `exclusiveMinimum`/`exclusiveMaximum` honoured in **both spellings** (3.0 boolean modifier + 3.1 number). (probe: `crust -c 'http :12345/nope \| sql --help'` prints usage, exit 0, no request) |
| U118 | L2021: `base64 [-d\|decode] [-f\|--file <path>] [-o\|--out <path>] [--]` — encode (default) or decode **text**: `echo hi \| base64` → `aGk=`, `echo aGk= \| base64 -d` → `hi`. Shadows `/usr/bin/base64`, which takes the same argument and encodes the **FILE** (see U119). Options are only ever what you typed — a `-d` arriving as a piped *item* is data; a dash you did not define is **refused, never encoded**. `-f`/`--file` reads a file's bytes, `-o`/`--out` writes the result to a file. Decoding checks input: a character outside `A-Za-z0-9+/=-_` and whitespace is an error, not mojibake | ? (probe: `printf '%s' -d \| base64` → `LWQ=`; bad char refused) |

So text stays the default, file is explicit `--file`, and when a typed operand *is* a file the shadow is called out **on stderr** (stdout unchanged) with the `--file` that means what the reader wanted. `--file` is also the only binary-safe route: `read logo.png \| base64` decodes the file as UTF-8 (every invalid byte → U+FFFD; measured on a 26-byte PNG, `77+9UE5H…` vs coreutils `iVBORw0KGgo…`); `--file` reads bytes, `--out` writes them | ? "Court"`); `source file.crust a b` hands args to the sourced file (`$0` = its own path), caller positionals restored on exit | ? Saying the path out loud: crust now prints a `note:` on stderr when the operand you typed *is* a file, and leaves stdout alone. **These two flags are current on `main`; they are not in the published release this page's grammar gate lints against, which is why they appear in prose here and not in a ` ```crust ` fence.**" (3) "Options are what you typed, never what the item says. It is `LWQ=` now — the two characters, encoded. The flip side is that a typo'd flag is refused instead of encoded: `base64 --desc hello` printed `LS1kZXNj` and exit 0, and now exits 1 naming the options it does have." (4) "Decoding checks its input. `not base64!!!` decoded to three replacement characters and exit 0, because Node's base64 decoder skips characters it does not know. Anything outside `A-Za-z0-9+/=`, base64url's `-` and `_`, and whitespace is now an error." (5) "`sort`/`head`/`wc` are ambiguous by design. Reading `… \| sort` as sh's is correct; reading `base64 hello` as `/usr/bin/base64` is not." (6) "A redirect never belongs to crust. (2) "**`filter` passes an empty stream through silently; `assert` fails on it.** That asymmetry is deliberate — it closes the \"SQL returned zero rows and the suite went green\" hole. `expect` also does not fail on empty." Measured across 4,138 APIs-guru specs: 2,274 put a keyword beside a `$ref`, 180 of them a structural one (2,003 sites — azure 104 specs, twilio 32; `type` 157, `nullable` 43), and of those 180, 118 are Swagger 2.0, 56 are openapi 3.0.x and only 6 are 3.1. (2) "**Run against the real server, not the mock.** The whole point is proving the server rejects; [mock-server](/docs/recipes/mock-server/) accepts anything that routes — unless you boot it with `--validate`, in which case it rejects with `422`, which still isn't your server's documented `400` contract." (three tips, each tied to an existing probe: (1) the regex is W395 row-1's discriminator — the same two-probe matrix (description matches → case; login-style description → no case) covers this restatement, no new probe; (2) the mock-parity claim — "accepts anything that routes" = a valid-ish POST to an unrouted-or-anything mock returns the spec's success, and with `--validate` a schema-violating body returns **422** (NOT 400) — probe both against the scratch mock (the 422-vs-400 distinction is the W307/W308 `--validate` probe family; the "which still isn't your server's documented 400 contract" half is the finding-prevention point: a generated 400 case run against a --validate mock would FAIL, which is the expected and honest behavior — no probe beyond capturing the 422); (3) the silence rule — an op documenting NO negative responses → zero cases (probe: a spec with only 200 documented → the op is absent from the generated file AND from any notice; the `note: N operation(s) left with no negative cases` aggregate (W405) is where they'd surface if they qualified by other means — an op with no 401/403/404 AND no body 400 simply doesn't exist in the output; capture what DOES happen: silence? notice?)) |

| W439 | L170: "**Rotation:** the follow loop tracks each file by inode and size. A rotate-and-recreate (logrotate, `mv app.log app.log.1 && touch app.log`) is detected via inode change, and the stream switches to the new file automatically. A truncate that shrinks the file below the current offset is also detected and resets the stream. A truncate-and-immediate-overwrite to a size ≥ the prior offset is indistinguishable from an append via `stat` alone, so it's treated as an append — matches GNU `tail -F` behavior." | ? (the THREE rotation cases, each a probe with a `tail -F` pipeline + a scripted appender: (1) rotate-and-recreate — `mv app.log app.log.1 && touch app.log`, then write to the NEW file: the tail picks up the new file's lines (the old file's un-tailed remainder is NOT re-tailed — the probe plants lines in the .1 after the mv and checks they are absent from the stream); (2) truncate-shrink — the appender truncates below the current offset: the stream RESETS (re-reads from 0? the "resets the stream" = subsequent appends are seen; probe: truncate to 0, append 2 lines → both seen, no duplicates of the pre-truncate content); (3) the acknowledged edge — truncate+overwrite to a size ≥ prior offset is TREATED AS AN APPEND (the page concedes the stat-only limitation; probe the behavior: overwrite in place at the same/larger size → the new content is partially or fully seen as an append, and the page's "indistinguishable" wording is the honest-disclosure claim — capture what actually happens and judge it against GNU `tail -F` doing the same); the "matches GNU tail -F" clause = run the SAME scenario under system `tail -F` in parallel and compare (the parity probe)) |

| W442 | L179–186 "## Live streams: two traps crust removes" → "`tail -F app.log \| grep ERROR` is the classic follow pipeline — and with system grep it silently stalls, because GNU grep block-buffers ~4KB when writing into a pipe. Crust runs the safe grep subset (`-i`/`-v`/`-F`, one pattern) as a **native line-buffered stage**, so every match streams the moment it arrives; anything fancier (combined flags, `$VARs`, POSIX classes, file grep) still gets exact system-grep semantics via `sh`." | ? (the buffering-trap claim — probe both halves: (1) the native line-buffered grep: `tail -F app.log | grep ERROR` under crust with a SLOW appender (one line every 500ms) → each match appears within the next poll (no 4KB stall; the observable: a match arrives <1s after being appended, not after ~4KB accumulate); (2) the safe subset: `grep -i ERROR`, `grep -v ERROR`, `grep -F "err"` (fixed string), one pattern each — all native (probe each streams line-buffered); (3) the fallback triggers: `grep -iE ERROR` (combined -iE), `grep "$PAT"` (variable), `grep '[0-9]{3}'` (POSIX-class-ish / brace), `grep ERROR somelog` (file argument) — each falls through to system grep via sh (probe: same semantics as system grep, i.e. the output equals `system grep` on the same input; and the buffering caveat applies to those — record that the fallback path RE-INTRODUCES the stall, which the page's "still gets exact system-grep semantics" admits); (4) "one pattern" = `grep ERROR WARN` (two patterns) is NOT in the safe subset (falls through — probe the discrimination); the ~4KB number is the GNU grep stdio buffer (record as stated; the observable is the stall, not the byte count)) |

(2) the alias `-` — `printf 'a\n' | crust -c '- | (l => l + "!")'` behaves the same (the alias is named explicitly — probe both spellings); (3) the try/catch-JSON pattern: a mixed stream (JSON lines + a banner line) → the banner becomes `null` (caught), `filter (e => e && …)` drops it (the `e &&` guard is the null-filter — probe a banner between JSON lines: no crash, the JSON lines still flow); (4) the stats capture: the "Real capture" lines show `stats --every 3` emitting rolling windows every 3s (the `window`, `count`, `wallMs` ≈3002, `rps`, `status` histogram, `p50/p99/meanMs` fields — the shape is the load-testing page's stats contract, W327 family; offline: a 6s trickle of lines → two windows with `wallMs` ≈3000 each, the count split; the "real capture" numbers (121/120, rps 40) are a HISTORICAL capture from a loaded container — NOT reproducible offline, record as stated, probe the SHAPE only); (5) the bare-pipe rule (L211–216) — FIVE distinct claims: (a) `cmd | crust` (NO -c) treats piped stdin as a SCRIPT and reads it to EOF before running (probe: `echo 'range(1,3)' | crust` → runs as a script, outputs 1 2 3; vs `echo 'range(1,3)' | crust -c 'stdin | (l => l)'` → the line is DATA; the two modes are the whole trap); (b) data pipes must go through `-c 'stdin | …'` (the examples are the proof); (c) a shell stage INSIDE a bare-pipe script sees EOF on its own stdin (probe: `echo 'x' | crust -c 'stdin | sh -c "cat"'`-style — the inner cat's stdin is… the page says the BARE-PIPE script's shell stages see EOF on their stdin — capture what a shell stage reading stdin inside `crust -c 'stdin | …'` actually gets); (d) `crust file.crust` does NOT drain stdin — its shell stages inherit the pipe and will consume it (probe: `printf 'a\nb\n' | crust script.crust` where script.crust has a `sh -c cat` stage → the shell stage eats the piped a/b; the file mode leaves stdin wired to shell stages); (e) "One stdin per run — the program, the `stdin` source, or the shell stages, never two" = a mutual-exclusion contract (probe: `printf 'a\n' | crust -c 'stdin | sh -c cat'` → ONE of them gets the data, not both; capture which wins and whether the other blocks/errors — the never-two claim means a conflict is defined, not silently duplicated)) |
| W444 | L218–223 "## Interactive: hold the stream, iterate on filters" → "Re-running a follow pipeline restarts the tail and loses the recent past. The `logs` builtin holds one live source open, buffers the last 10k items (`--buffer N` to change), and every line you type is an ordinary pipeline fragment run over that buffer first, then over the live stream:" | ? (the logs-session contract — the interactive probe needs a pty (the W333+ logs-page probe family via `script -qec`): (1) "holds one live source open" — start `logs <cmd>` under a pty, the source runs ONCE (the boot line names it, W446); (2) "buffers the last 10k items" — the default buffer size 10000 (the boot message's "buffering last 10000 items", W446; and the `buffer` command shows the count, W446); (3) `--buffer N` — `logs --buffer 5 --tail ... <cmd>`-style (the flag ON the logs builtin, not per query — probe: boot with a small buffer, the boot message says `buffering last 5 items`? or is --buffer parsed differently — capture the boot line verbatim); (4) "every line you type is an ordinary pipeline fragment" — a typed line is parsed as pipeline grammar (probe: `grep x`, a lambda, `filter (…)` all accepted as typed lines — the W343+ session-command vs pipeline-fragment discrimination); (5) "run over that buffer first, then over the live stream" — the double-run: a typed query emits the buffer matches FIRST, then a `-- live --` separator, then live matches (the transcript W446 shows exactly this; probe: append lines WHILE a query is live — they appear after the `-- live --` marker, and a NEW query re-runs over the buffer again (the buffer persists across queries)) |
| W445 | L225–229 three boot forms: `logs docker logs -f --tail 300 my-app     # any shell command's stdout` / `logs tail -n 0 -F app.log                 # or a file follow` / `logs procs({web: "bun run dev"})          # or a whole process group` | ? (the three source kinds — probe each under a pty: (1) the shell-command form: `logs echo hi` or `logs sh -c 'while true; do echo tick; sleep 1; done'` — any command whose stdout is held (the `--tail 300` is part of the COMMAND, not a logs flag — the page's example passes docker's own flags; probe that logs' own flag parsing does NOT swallow the command's flags: does `logs tail -n 0 -F app.log` parse `-n`/`-F` as tail's, not logs' — a flag-ambiguity probe, the `--`-live marker family, W343(2)); (2) the file-follow form: `logs tail -n 0 -F app.log` — the boot line names it (the logs page W344 captured `tail -n 0 -F app.log — buffering last 10000 items` — cross-check the EXACT boot format between the two pages, parity family); (3) the procs form: `logs procs({web: "bun run dev"})` — a process GROUP as the source (the boot message for a procs source, W344's procs note `items are {proc, stream, line} objects` — the logs page already carries it; here the cross-ref is that the SAME builtin accepts a procs spec at boot) |
| W446 | L231–253 "A real session against a live container:" → transcript: `logs: docker logs -f --tail 300 my-app — buffering last 10000 items` / `logs> grep api_request` / `{"level":30,"msg":"api_request","path":"/api/health","status":200,"duration_ms":1}` / `…                       ← matches from the buffer (the recent past)` / `-- live --` / `…                       ← new matches as they arrive` / `^C` / `logs> json on` / `json: on — string items parsing to JSON objects/arrays reach queries parsed` / `logs[json]> filter (e => e.status) \| (e => e.path + " " + e.duration_ms + "ms")` / `-- live --` / `/api/health 1ms` / `/api/health 0ms` / `^C` / `logs[json]> search timeout` / `search: 2 matching item(s) of 551 buffered` / `logs[json]> buffer 50000` / `buffer: resized 10000 → 50000 (kept 551 item(s))` / `logs[json]> exit` | ? (the REAL-SESSION transcript — every line is a verbatim capture target (the pty probe replays this exact session against a scratch JSON emitter): (1) the boot line format `logs: <command> — buffering last <N> items` (em-dash, count, the command echoed); (2) the `logs>` prompt, then `logs[json]>` AFTER json on (the prompt CHANGES to show json mode — capture both spellings); (3) the double-run output: buffer matches, then the literal `-- live --` separator, then live matches (the separator is a printed line, verbatim, with the surrounding blanks); (4) `^C` in the transcript = the user's Ctrl-C (W450's graceful live-view end); (5) `json: on — string items parsing to JSON objects/arrays reach queries parsed` — the json-on acknowledgment line VERBATIM (em-dash); (6) the filtered projection output `/api/health 1ms` (the lambda `(e => e.path + " " + e.duration_ms + "ms")` applied to PARSED items — the json-on mode is what lets the lambda see `.status`/`.path` without an explicit parse stage); (7) `search: 2 matching item(s) of <M> buffered` — the search line format with the buffered count (M varies — the transcript's 551 is that session's count; the FORMAT is the claim: `search: <n> matching item(s) of <m> buffered`); (8) `buffer: resized 10000 → 50000 (kept 551 item(s))` — the resize line VERBATIM (arrow, the kept count); (9) `exit` tears down (W450). The transcript's numbers (121/551/2) are the session's actual values — probe for FORMATS, not values; the `←` annotations in the page's block are the page's OWN comments inside the transcript (they are not printed by logs — the probe must confirm logs does NOT print `← matches from the buffer`, else the transcript is a misrepresentation)) |
| W447 | L255–262: "`json on` is the session's honest answer to NDJSON streams: parsing happens at query time (the buffer stays raw, `json off` reverts instantly), lines that aren't JSON objects flow through unchanged **and are counted out loud** — so a mixed stream can neither crash a query (the old `(l => JSON.parse(l))` lambda threw on the first plain line) nor silently lose data. `search <text>` answers instantly from the buffer with a match count (zero included) and highlighting; `buffer N` widens the window without restarting the held source." | ? (the json-mode contract — pty probes: (1) "parsing happens at query time, the buffer stays raw" — after `json on`, a query like `grep health` matches against the PARSED view (field access works) but the BUFFERED content is still the raw strings (probe: `json on` → a query with a lambda using `.level` works → `json off` → the same lambda now receives STRINGS (the `e.level` is undefined on a string — the behavior change is observable: a query that worked under json-on errors or mismatches after json-off, captured verbatim); (2) "lines that aren't JSON objects flow through unchanged AND are counted out loud" — a mixed stream (a banner line among JSON): under json-on, the banner flows through as-is (the query sees it as a string) AND it is COUNTED (where is the count printed? the search/buffer lines? the W446 transcript's "551 buffered" includes non-JSON lines? probe: plant 5 JSON + 1 banner, the count is 6, and a json-on query returns the banner unchanged as one item — the "counted out loud" mechanism is the probe's question: is there a per-query notice like `1 item(s) not JSON`? capture whatever the count line is); (3) "zero included" search — `search zzznomatch` → `search: 0 matching item(s) of <M> buffered` (the zero line format); (4) "highlighting" — the search output highlights matches (ANSI in the pty capture — the highlight markers are ANSI, capture and note; the page's transcript does NOT show a search result line, only the count — the highlighting claim is unshown in the transcript, probe it); (5) `buffer N` "without restarting the held source" — the W446 resize line proves the source kept running (the kept count 551 = the buffer content survived; the live stream continues — probe: append a line after the resize, it arrives (the source is alive)) |
| W448 | L264–273: "Because every query is ordinary pipeline grammar, rendering is just another stage — filter on the raw JSON first, then hand the matches to `pino-pretty`:" → transcript: `logs> grep api_request \| pino-pretty --colorize --singleLine` / `[11:46:18.313] INFO: api_request {"request_id":"…","path":"/api/health","status":200,"duration_ms":4}` / `-- live --` / `…colorized lines keep streaming until ^C…` | ? (the rendering-as-stage claim — pty probe: (1) a typed query CAN include a shell stage (`pino-pretty` is a shell stage after `grep`) — the session accepts mixed-grammar lines (the "ordinary pipeline grammar" claim at its fullest: grep + external binary in one typed line); (2) pino-pretty must be RESOLVED — it is an npm binary (the page's next paragraph W449 explains the PATH; the probe needs pino-pretty installed in a scratch node_modules ancestor OR the command form fails with a not-found (which is itself a finding about the claim's precondition — the page assumes the tool is installed, record that); (3) the output line format `[11:46:18.313] INFO: api_request {…}` — pino-pretty's own output (the `[ts] LEVEL: message {json}` shape — capture from a real run and confirm the page's line is pino-pretty's genuine format, not a stylization); (4) the `-- live --` separator appears in THIS query's output too (the double-run applies to shell-stage queries as well); (5) the double-run subtlety: a shell stage over the buffer means pino-pretty runs over the buffer items FIRST (the transcript's first output line is a buffer match) — and a shell stage RESTARTS per run (the pino-pretty process is spawned for the buffer pass, then again for live? or once? the "every query is ordinary pipeline grammar" reading says per-pass; probe: does the pino-pretty process appear twice in `ps` during a query? — a subtle implementation observable, mark as best-effort)) |
| W449 | L275–279: "No `node_modules/.bin/` prefix needed: shell stages get npm-run-style PATH — every ancestor `node_modules/.bin` is prepended, so locally-installed tools work bare. `--colorize` forces ANSI even though pino-pretty runs behind a pipe. Keep the `grep`/`filter` **before** the pretty stage — after it, you'd be matching against ANSI-decorated text." | ? (three claims: (1) the PATH prepend — probe: a scratch dir with `node_modules/.bin/` containing a marker script (an executable printing `MARKER`), run a pipeline with a shell stage calling that script BARE from a cwd with that ancestor → the script resolves (and from a cwd WITHOUT the ancestor → not found, the negative control); "every ancestor" = multiple levels up each contribute (probe two nested node_modules/.bin dirs, both resolvable); (2) `--colorize` forces ANSI behind a pipe — the pty probe's capture: even though stdout is the session's pipe, the output has ANSI color codes (the `--colorize` flag's job; the page claims the stage runs "behind a pipe" — i.e. not a tty — so pino-pretty would NOT colorize without the flag; probe WITHOUT --colorize: plain text; WITH: ANSI — the on/off pair is the evidence); (3) the ordering advice — a grep AFTER pino-pretty matches ANSI-decorated text (probe: the same grep before vs after — before: N matches on the raw JSON; after: the match count changes or the match is against escape codes (a grep for a substring that exists in the JSON but is split by an ANSI code fails — the concrete demonstration of "matching against ANSI-decorated text")) |
| W450 | L281–286: "The parts worth knowing: **Ctrl-C once** ends the live view *gracefully* — the stream finishes, so a query ending in bare `stats` prints its summary exactly there; **Ctrl-C twice** hard-cancels a stuck query; the session and the held source survive both. A fragment runs twice (buffer, then live), so append with `>>`, never `>`. `exit`/Ctrl-D tears the held source down — `procs` groups get the SIGTERM→SIGKILL escalation." | ? (the session control semantics — the pty probe matrix (this is the W349/Ctrl-C ×3 family at recipe depth): (1) Ctrl-C ONCE on a live query → the live view ends, the prompt returns, the held source KEEPS running (probe: a query live, Ctrl-C, then a NEW query sees the lines that arrived DURING the cancelled query (they were buffered — the source never stopped); (2) "a query ending in bare `stats` prints its summary exactly there" — a `… | stats` query under Ctrl-C: the stats SUMMARY line is printed at the Ctrl-C (the graceful flush — the W326 family's stats-final contract in the session; probe: a stats query, let it accumulate, Ctrl-C → the final summary line appears, THEN the prompt); (3) Ctrl-C TWICE (quickly) → hard-cancel of a STUCK query (probe: a query that would not end on its own (a shell stage reading a held-open fd, or a `sleep`-long command), single Ctrl-C does not kill it (it's mid-stage), the second Ctrl-C cancels the query (the stage process is killed — `ps` shows the stage's children gone); "the session and the held source survive both" = after the double-Ctrl-C the prompt is back AND the held source still streams (a new grep sees fresh lines); (4) "append with `>>`, never `>`" — the double-run means a `>` redirect inside a typed fragment would be TRUNCATED TWICE (buffer pass, then live pass) — the page's advice is that shell redirects in session queries should be `>>` (probe: is a `>` in a typed line even allowed? the W250/W364 top-level-redirect-refusal family — if a typed `> file` is refused outright, the advice is moot; if it works, the double-truncation is real — capture which); (5) `exit`/Ctrl-D → the held source is torn down: for a shell-command source the process group dies (ps shows nothing), for a `procs` source the SIGTERM→SIGKILL escalation (W359's probe serves both pages — the grandchild-survival check); (6) the `exit` command vs Ctrl-D equivalence (both tear down — the transcript W446 uses `exit`; probe Ctrl-D (EOF byte) does the same)) |
| W451 | L288–292 Tips (three bullets): (1) "**Mix `grep` and lambdas** — `grep` is unbeatable for plain substring/regex filters; reserve lambdas for things grep can't see (JSON shape, async lookups, multi-line state)." (2) "**`lines` vs `read` vs `Bun.file().text()`** — the shell stage `read <glob>` yields **whole-file** items (one per file), `lines <glob>` yields **one item per line**, and the TypeScript `read(path)` streams lines like the latter. They print identically on a terminal, so the difference only shows when a downstream `filter`/lambda sees one giant string instead of lines. `grep` masks it by splitting internally — which is why `read **/*.log \| grep ERROR \| filter (…)` works and swapping those last two stages silently does not." (3) "**Pair with `time \"label\"`** to know how long a forensic query takes — the timer fires even if a downstream stage throws." | ? (three tips; (1) is prose over already-probed stages (R, cross-refs W442/W431); (2) is the SHARPEST tip and answers W430's read-contract question: the shell stage `read <glob>` yields WHOLE-FILE items (one item per file — the string is the entire file content), `lines <glob>` yields one item per line, and the TS `read(path)` streams LINES (not whole files!) — this CONTRADICTS W379's read contract ("`read` yields each file's contents as one item") IF W379's `read` is the same function: the verify-web-links page's `read report.json | (s => JSON.parse(s))` relies on the whole-file read, and the W379 row probed it as "contents as one item" — so the page now claims THREE different read behaviors by dialect (shell read = whole file; lines = per-line; TS read = per-line) — PROBE ALL THREE on the same 2-line file: `read f.txt` in a bash pipeline (item count downstream = 1, the whole text), `lines f.txt` (2 items), `bun script.ts` with `await read("f.txt").collect()` (the TS half — 2 line items? or 1? the page says "streams lines like the latter" = 2); the "print identically" clause = both forms print the same bytes to a terminal (probe: `read f.txt` and `lines f.txt` piped to nothing, terminal output identical); the `grep`-masks clause = `read **/*.log | grep ERROR` WORKS (grep internally splits the whole-file item into lines and matches — the item after grep is the matching LINES, so `| filter (…)` sees strings that are lines, not files) while `read **/*.log | filter (l => l.includes('ERROR')) | grep ERROR` does NOT (the filter sees ONE giant string per file — it keeps or drops the WHOLE file; the "silently" is the trap: no error, just wrong results — probe the swap: the swapped pipeline's output is the whole files that contain ERROR, or nothing, and NO error is raised); (3) the `time "label"` stage — probe: `time "myquery" | …`-style (the stage names a label, measures the downstream, prints the elapsed at the END; "even if a downstream stage throws" = the timer's print is in a finally — probe: `time "x" | sh -c 'exit 1'` → the elapsed line still prints AND the pipeline still fails (exit nonzero + both the error and the timer line, capture the order)) |
| W452 | L294–297 Related (two links): [Intro](/docs/intro/) "refresher on sources, transforms, sinks" · [Quickstart](/docs/quickstart/) "every source/transform/sink in one tour" | R (internal links covered by check-links; both targets already audited (docs/intro, docs/quickstart in the 20/29 done list) — the two cross-ref descriptions are generic tour claims, no new probes; log-mining.mdx is now COMPLETE: W427–W452 over 297 L) |

#### recipes/api-smoke-tests.mdx (412 L, read in 160+160+92)
| # | Claim (verbatim, line-anchored) | Status |
|---|---|---|
| W453 | L1–5 frontmatter: `title: API smoke tests`; description: "One-liners, capture-chained CRUD `.pipes` suites with DB asserts and negative blocks, and `.crust.ts` fixtures — API smoke tests at three altitudes." | R (human title, recipe style; "three altitudes" = the L11–15 bullet list, each with its section below) |
| W454 | L9–15: "**Goal:** prove an HTTP service does what it claims — creates persist, reads round-trip, deletes tombstone, and the API *rejects* what it must — with pipelines instead of a test framework." + "This recipe works at three altitudes. Pick by how much each test needs to know:" + bullets: "**One shell line** — the whole test is self-contained: body, headers, one assertion." / "**A `.pipes` file** — a *suite* of shell lines, run in order, chained with `capture`, with SQL/`assert` lines checking what earlier lines did." / "**`.crust.ts` fixtures (the TypeScript API)** — a single test needs multi-step logic: generated tokens, per-fixture setup/teardown, structural matchers." | R (goal + altitude ladder prose; each bullet's named mechanism has its section: one-liner L17, .pipes L81, fixtures later on the page — the "rejects what it must" half is the negative-block L104 + gen-fixtures cross-ref) |
| W455 | L17–36 "## The one-liner" → bash: `# read yields each file's CONTENTS (a bare glob would POST the paths)` + `read fixtures/*.json \| POST :3000/users \| expect 201` · ts: `await readAll("fixtures/*.json").pipe(POST("http://localhost:3000/users")).pipe(expectStage(201)).collect();` + comment "On mismatch, expectStage rejects with ExpectError { item, index, matcher }." | ? (the one-liner, both halves, against a scratch POST-echo server returning 201: (1) the `read` CONTENTS contract — `read fixtures/*.json` yields ONE ITEM PER FILE = the whole contents (the W451(2)/W379 read family, asserted again here with the explicit negative: "a bare glob would POST the paths" — probe a bare `glob`-style source or a `cat`-less form to confirm the page's contrast is real: what does a plain path-yielding source do as a POST body? the paths themselves); (2) `POST :3000/users` — the `:port/path` URL form (W363's wait-target family: the `:port` shorthand for localhost); each item becomes ONE request body (a 2-file fixture dir → exactly 2 requests, bodies = file contents verbatim — the scratch server logs bodies); (3) `expect 201` — the expect stage on status (a 200 from the server → pipeline FAILS, exit 1, "naming the mismatch count" per L38 — the mismatch output format is W456's probe); (4) the ts half: `readAll` (a DIFFERENT name from bash `read` and from the ts `read` of W451(2)! — three read spellings across the pages: bash `read` = whole-file, ts `read` = lines, ts `readAll` = whole-file; the ts API surface must actually export `readAll` — CODE REVIEW: grep src/ for `readAll` and check the exports; `POST(url)` as a stage factory, `expectStage(201)` as a stage FACTORY (the TS names: `expect` is a reserved word? — the page's ts half uses `expectStage`, a name the bash world doesn't have; probe the ts half in a .crust script: does `expectStage(201)` exist and does it reject with an `ExpectError` carrying `{item, index, matcher}` (the comment claims the reject SHAPE — a deliberately mismatched fixture (expect 201 against a 200) → the rejection's fields, captured verbatim)) |
| W456 | L38–44: "`read fixtures/*.json` is a pipeline with one item per matched file — the file's **whole contents**, which `POST :3000/users` sends as the request body, per item. `expect 201` drains the stream and fails the pipeline (exit 1) naming the mismatch count. When the exact status doesn't matter, class matchers work too: `expect 2xx` accepts anything 200–299 (`3xx`, `4xx`, `5xx` likewise). Add auth with `-H` (URLs, `-H` values and JSON literals are `$VAR` env-expanded):" + bash `read fixtures/*.json \| POST $BASE/api/users -H "authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \| expect 201` + L44 "Reach for the one-liner when the test has no memory: fire, assert, done. The moment a later request needs a value from an earlier response, move up a rung." | ? (five sub-claims on the scratch server: (1) one item per matched file, whole contents, body per item = W455(1)/(2) — the combined probe; (2) `expect` DRAINS the stream (expect with no further stage = terminal: the pipeline's output is the expect verdict, not the items — probe: `read f.json | POST … | expect 201` prints NOTHING on success? and on failure the mismatch output; "drains" = it consumes all items before failing, so a 3-file dir with 1 bad status → the mismatch COUNT is reported (probe 2 good + 1 bad → the count says 1, and BOTH good ones were sent (drain-before-verdict, not fail-fast-at-first) — capture the count line verbatim); (3) class matchers: `expect 2xx` accepts 200 AND 299 (probe both extremes + 300 → fail; `3xx`/`4xx`/`5xx` likewise — the 4xx class against a 404 server passes); (4) `-H` adds a header (the scratch server sees `authorization: Bearer …` exactly; `$BASE`/`$TOKEN` env-expanded in the URL and the -H value — the expansion-claim preview of W463(2): set BASE/TOKEN in the env, run, the server logs the expanded values); (5) L44 is prose (R, the altitude ladder's rungs)) |
| W457 | L46–57 "## Request chaining — `capture`" → L48: "`capture NAME (fn)` runs `fn` on each item and writes the result to `process.env.NAME`. Because crust parses each line right before running it, **every later line sees `$NAME`** — in the REPL, in `crust -c` scripts, and in `.pipes` files. That one primitive turns \"a pile of one-liners\" into \"a flow\"." → L52–55 login example (`crust -c '… POST :3000/api/login | assert (r => r.status === 200) | (r => r.json()) | capture TOKEN (b => b.token)` / `GET :3000/api/things -H "authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" | expect 200'`) → L57: "Line 1 logs in, parses the body, and captures the token. Line 2 — a *separate* pipeline — expands `$TOKEN` like any env var. Captures happen at **run** time and `$VAR` expansion at **parse** time, which is why chaining works *across* lines but never within one." | ? (the capture primitive — probe against a scratch login endpoint (a server answering the login with `{token: "t-123"}`): (1) the mechanics — `capture TOKEN (b => b.token)` runs fn on EACH item (a 2-item upstream → fn twice, the LAST write wins? or an error on multiple? the page says "writes the result" — multi-item behavior is a probe: does the 2nd overwrite silently?); (2) the cross-line visibility — the login line + a 2nd line using `$TOKEN` in a URL and a `-H` value: the server sees `Bearer t-123` (the expansion is real, in `-c` scripts; the same in a `.pipes` file = W460's suite, and "in the REPL" = the interactive crust session — the pty probe: capture in one REPL line, `$TOKEN` in the next, it works); (3) the RUN-vs-PARSE timing claim — the SHARPEST claim: `$VAR` expansion is at PARSE time (each line is parsed right before it runs), captures at RUN time — so a capture EARLIER IN THE SAME LINE is NOT visible later in that line (probe: `{"x":1} | POST :3000/t | (r => r.json()) | capture ID (b => "abc") | GET :3000/t/$ID | expect 200` on ONE line → `$ID` is expanded from the pre-run env (empty/absent — the URL becomes `:3000/t/` or the literal is kept? capture verbatim what the server sees and whether the line fails); the across-lines direction works (the login example is the proof); (4) the `assert (r => r.status === 200)` BEFORE the json parse in the login line (the status gate then the body extract — the order matters: an assert-fail skips the parse; probe a 500 login → the assert fails, no json parse error (the assert-first ordering is protective)) |
| W458 | L59–64: "Two honesty rules keep captures from lying to you:" + bullet: "A **nullish captured value fails the pipeline immediately** — `crust: capture TOKEN: got undefined from (b => b.token) — item 1` — instead of expanding to a baffling `\"\"` three lines later." + bullet: "An **empty upstream also fails**. A capture that captured nothing is a bug, not a pass." + L64: "Mind the names: capturing into `TOKEN`, `BASE`, or `PATH` overwrites those for the rest of the run." | ? (the two honesty rules, each a probe: (1) nullish — a login whose body has NO token field (the scratch server answers `{}`) → the pipeline fails IMMEDIATELY on the capture line, the message VERBATIM: `crust: capture TOKEN: got undefined from (b => b.token) — item 1` (the `undefined` vs `null` distinction: a missing field gives `undefined`, a `null` field value presumably gives `null` in the same message — probe both; the "— item 1" = the item index (a 2-item upstream failing on the 2nd → "item 2"? probe the numbering); the NEGATIVE half: the failure is on the CAPTURE line, so a later line never runs (fail-fast, no `""` expansion three lines later — the observable: the script stops, exit 1, no downstream request logged by the server); (2) empty upstream — `… | capture TOKEN (b => b.token)` where the upstream stream is EMPTY (a filter that drops everything, or a read of a nonexistent-ish glob that matches nothing) → the capture FAILS (the message verbatim — is it the same nullish message or a distinct "empty" message? the page implies a distinct failure mode; capture both the message and the exit code); (3) L64's name warning — capturing into `TOKEN`, `BASE`, `PATH` overwrites them FOR THE REST OF THE RUN (probe: a pre-set `BASE` env used by line 1, a capture of `BASE` on line 2, then line 3's `$BASE` is the captured value (the overwrite is real and run-scoped — and does it persist AFTER the `-c` run? | W490 | L93–104 (string bullet, part 1): "`string` → a value that satisfies the schema's own constraints: a format-aware default for `email`, `date-time`, `uuid`, `uri`; otherwise a value constructed from `pattern` — character classes, `\\d \\w \\s .`, the quantifiers `{n} {n,m} + * ?`, `\\uXXXX` escapes inside a class (`[\\u0031-\\u0039]` **is** `[1-9]`, and AWS writes it that way), literals, and **groups**: `(?:…)` is read as the ordinary group it is, an alternation inside a group takes its first buildable branch, and a group counts as one atom so `(…){n}`, `(…)*`, `(…)+` and `(…)?` repeat what the group matched. That is the difference between a placeholder and a value for the shapes specs really write — `^(subnet-[0-9a-f]{8}\|subnet-[0-9a-f]{17})$` yields `subnet-00000000`, `^([0-9]{1,3}\\.){3}[0-9]{1,3}$` yields `0.0.0.0`, and the base64 content pattern yields `AA==`. All of it **verified against the real regex before it is used**, so a construction crust cannot manage degrades to a neutral value rather than a confidently wrong one — reading more can cost a build that should have worked, never a value that is wrong. Alternation is never **joined**: `(a\|b)` builds one branch, never `a\|b`, because the match is an unanchored test — a joined value passes it and breaks the `maxLength` beside it." | ? (the string generator — probe battery, each a scratch spec schema, asserting the SERVED value: (1) format-aware defaults — `{type: string, format: email}` → a syntactically valid email (the probe validates the served string against the email grammar); (3) the `\\uXXXX` escape claim — a pattern containing `[\\u0031-\\u0039]` (the AWS style) → the served digit is in 1–9 (the escape is DECODED, not copied literally — a served value containing the literal text `\\u0031` would be the failure); if it is not `AA==` under the canonical pattern, the claim is recorded as stated with the pattern-identity caveat — the `AA==` output is the minimum-length padding case (the builder picks the shortest valid base64: two A's + the `==` pad)); a group as an atom: `(ab){3}` → `ababab` (the group repeats as a unit, not one char of it); (6) the verification clause — "All of it verified against the real regex before it is used" (the degradation direction: a pattern the builder cannot satisfy within its supported subset → the served value is a NEUTRAL value (a plain string, the W491 fallback), NOT a confidently-wrong one — the probe uses one of W491's declined shapes and confirms the served value is neutral + `--validate` reports it (the W491(6) probe is the shared exhibit)); the "reading more can cost a build that should have worked, never a value that is wrong" = the asymmetry thesis (R, the design rule behind the degradation) |
| W491 | L105–121 (string bullet, part 2): "A pipe inside a character class (`[\\w\|-]`) is just one of its members and is built normally. Values are then clamped to `minLength` / `maxLength` — but **never in a way that unmakes the match just verified**. A length is bought by widening a quantifier that was already open-ended (`+`, `*`, `{n,}`); a fixed-width `{3}` is never widened, since that would be the same mistake in a new place. Where a pattern and a length bound cannot both hold, the pattern wins — it is the narrower statement about the field — and `--validate` still reports the length violation, so the spec's contradiction stays visible. A **format-derived value is likewise never truncated**. A schema saying `{format: uuid, maxLength: 8}` contradicts itself, since a uuid is 36 characters; crust keeps the valid uuid rather than emitting `\"00000000\"`, which would trade a `maxLength` violation for a `format` one and lose the useful value on the way. Lengthening still applies: padding an email to `minLength` is harmless where truncating a uuid is not. Plain `\"string\"` appears only when nothing constrains the field. What crust still declines — a lookahead or lookbehind, a named or atomic group, a back-reference, nesting past eight levels, `\\A`/`\\Z` (Python anchors, which ECMA-262 reads as the letter `A`), or a pattern written as a JavaScript regex literal (`/^[a-z0-9._]+$/`, where JSON Schema takes the leading slash as a literal and nothing can satisfy it) — falls back to a neutral value rather than a guess: knowingly wrong beats confidently wrong, and `--validate` reports it." | ? (clamping + the decline list — probe battery: (1) the in-class pipe — a pattern `^[\\w\|-]+$` → the served value matches the pattern (the `|` is a member, buildable — the value MAY contain a literal `|` (a member) or not (another member chosen); the claim is no misparse: the probe asserts self-match, and separately that the builder did NOT treat `[\\w|-]` as an alternation (a value matching `\\w` only is fine, a value containing a group boundary would be the bug)); (2) the clamp-without-unmaking — a pattern `^[a-z]+$` with `minLength: 5` → the served value is ≥5 chars (the length is BOUGHT by widening the open-ended `+`), and still matches; `maxLength: 7` on the same → ≤7; (3) the fixed-width never-widened — `^[a-z]{3}$` with `minLength: 5` (the contradiction) → the served value is length 3 (the PATTERN wins — the value matches the pattern, the length bound is violated on purpose); the visibility half — "and `--validate` still reports the length violation": the probe feeds the served value back through a `--stateful --validate` boot (POST the generated value to the matching request schema → the 422 names the length violation — the contradiction "stays visible" via the validator, the probe captures the 422 body verbatim); (4) the format-never-truncated — `{format: uuid, maxLength: 8}` → the served value is a FULL 36-char uuid (NOT `"00000000"` — the probe asserts length 36 and uuid validity; the page's named anti-behavior (truncating to 8) must be absent); the lengthening twin — `{format: email, minLength: 60}` → the served email is padded to ≥60 (still a valid email — padding is "harmless": probe the padding character/shape (a longer local part?) and the email validity); (5) the plain-string floor — a bare `{type: string}` (no format, no pattern, no bounds) → the served value is the plain string `\"string\"` (the literal word? the probe captures the exact value — "Plain `\"string\"` appears only when nothing constrains the field" reads as the literal string value `string` (the neutral value), captured verbatim); (6) the DECLINE list (each a probe schema, each expected to yield a NEUTRAL value (the plain string) rather than a guess, with `--validate` reporting): a lookahead `(?!abc)` or lookbehind `(?<=a)`; a named group `(?<id>\\d+)` or atomic group `(?>a)`; a back-reference `(a)\\1`; nesting past eight levels (nine nested groups — the boundary probe: 8 levels builds, 9 declines? the "past eight" = 9+ declines, 8 builds — probe both boundaries if time allows, else the 9-level case); `\\A`/`\\Z` anchors (a pattern `^\\Aabc\\Z$` — the ECMA-262 reading: `\\A` is the letter A, so the pattern is effectively `^AabcZ$`-ish — the served value must NOT be a guess satisfying the mangled reading (the neutral fallback is correct because the pattern-as-written is unsatisfiable under ECMA semantics — wait: `\\A` as the letter A means the pattern `\\Aabc` = literal "Aabc" — that IS satisfiable! the decline is about the PYTHON intent (start-of-string) — the page says these are declined: probe a pattern with `\\A` and record: neutral value (declined) — the probe's job is to confirm the DECLINE happens, per the page's list, regardless of the satisfiability subtlety); the JS-regex-literal pattern `/^[a-z0-9._]+$/` (the leading slash as a literal — the pattern requires a literal `/` then the class… the page says "nothing can satisfy it" (the trailing `$` inside + the leading `/` literal — a value would need to start with `/` AND match `^[a-z0-9._]+$` with its `^`… under JSON-Schema-as-ECMA the pattern is the string `/^[a-z0-9._]+$/` = literal `/`, then `^` anchor mid-pattern (matches only at start — after the `/` we are at index 1, `^` fails) → unsatisfiable → the builder must decline (the verification clause catches it: the builder's candidate can't match → neutral value, `--validate` reports) — the probe confirms the neutral output) |

| W498 | L176–182 (rule 4): "A body expands at most **20,000 schema nodes**. Past that a `$ref` to anything that can expand further terminates exactly as a cycle does. Truncation leaves a finite body and mock-server says so on stderr, but **a truncated body is not guaranteed to satisfy its own schema**: termination follows the same element rule as a cycle, so where the budget runs out on a required array whose element type has requirements of its own, the value is `[]` against its own `minItems`. Run `--validate` to see where. The warning used to claim validity, which told the operator not to look for it. Some real graphs are *mutually* recursive — presalytics.io/ooxml has `Slide.Slides.Details` referencing `Shared.*.Details` referencing back — and with per-path cycle detection the cost of a complete body grows with the number of **paths**, not nodes: 52 million expansions for 134 responses and 53 seconds for that one file, which from outside is a hang." | ? (3) the minItems-honesty corner — the budget running out "on a required array whose element type has requirements of its own" → the value is `[]` AGAINST ITS OWN minItems (an empty array where minItems demands more — the deliberate visible violation; the probe: the truncated body has the `[]`, and `--validate` run against the spec flags exactly that minItems violation (the "Run --validate to see where" half — the probe runs the validator on the truncated body and captures the named violation); (4) the mutual-recursion history (R — presalytics.io/ooxml, `Slide.Slides.Details` ↔ `Shared.*.Details`, 52 million expansions / 134 responses / 53 seconds — not reproducible offline without that file; the behavioral half: a scratch MUTUALLY recursive pair (A ref B, B ref A) terminates (the per-node/per-visit detection handles the two-node cycle — the probe: the body is finite, no hang, the cycle termination shapes per W499) |
| W499 | L183–208 (rule 5): "Local `$ref`s into `components.schemas.*` are resolved, and **narrowing keywords written beside a `$ref` apply** — `enum`, `format`, `pattern`, the bounds, `example`/`examples`, `default` — since OpenAPI 3.1 permits them and they are how a spec restricts one use of a shared type. **Structural** siblings (`type`, `properties`, `items`, `required`, `nullable`, `allOf`/`oneOf`/`anyOf`) are deliberately ignored: sibling keywords were illegal beside `$ref` before 3.1, so Swagger-2 conversions are full of leftovers the tools of that era ignored, and acting on them makes correct bodies wrong. `gen-fixtures` inlines a `$ref` by this same rule, so both halves of crust read one referring node the same way — the britbox and digitalocean numbers are in the gen-fixtures page. A **cyclic** `$ref` terminates with the properties its schema REQUIRES and nothing else. Dropping the optional ones is what makes it finite, since the property that closes the loop is nearly always optional — a slot may contain a sub-slot. A required property that closes the loop gets the empty shape of the type it declares. A required **array** of the type being terminated is finite either way, and which answer is right depends on the ELEMENT. Where the element type requires nothing of its own, the array is filled to `minItems` with that empty shape — it costs nothing and satisfies the bound, and connect's evaluation forms need exactly this (`EvaluationFormItem` requires nothing, so a terminated section keeps its `minItems: 1`). Where the element has required properties, the array stays `[]`: one element carrying none of them is valid at the top and wrong at every level below, so `--validate` reports the bound rather than crust hiding it behind a value that only looks right. quicksight's sheet layouts are that case — `Layout` requires `Configuration`. Both the required list and that type are read **through `allOf`** — real specs compose recursive types out of branches rather than declaring them on the node (bitbucket's `comment.parent` and `commit.parents` are the canonical case), and reading the node alone found nothing and terminated with `null` — `{}` for an object, `[]` for an array, read from `type` or, where a spec omits it, from the presence of `properties`/`items`. It used to terminate with `null`, which stopped the recursion just as well but put the wrong type in the body, so crust's own validator rejected it." | ? the narrowed generation gives `x` — the probe asserts membership); one probe per sibling class: `format` beside a ref (a string ref + sibling `format: email` → an email), `pattern` (a value matching the sibling pattern), a bound (a `minimum` beside a number ref → ≥ the minimum), `example`/`examples` (a sibling example beside a ref → the example wins (the W488 rule-1 priority survives the ref — the example is served verbatim)), `default` (a sibling default beside a ref — the probe records whether the default is used as the value or ignored (the page lists it among the applying keywords — the observable: the generated value equals the default? capture)); (2) the structural siblings are IGNORED — `{$ref: Base, type: integer}` (a STRUCTURAL sibling beside a string ref) → the value is a STRING (the ref's type wins, the sibling `type` is dropped — the deliberate-ignorance probe: acting on it would make the body an integer (wrong per the spec's pre-3.1-illegal leftover logic)); likewise a sibling `properties` beside a ref is ignored (the object shape comes from the ref alone); (3) the gen-fixtures parity — "gen-fixtures inlines a $ref by this same rule" (the cross-system contract: the SAME referring node generates the SAME value in gen-fixtures' output and the mock's body — the probe: a spec with the sibling-narrowing ref above, run through BOTH the mock and gen-fixtures (a minimal case) → the values agree (the shared-inlining claim, the W397/W421 gen-fixtures family); the britbox/digitalocean numbers (R, history, on the gen-fixtures page — already audited W387–W426)); (4) the cyclic termination — a self-recursive component (`Slot: {type: object, properties: {name: {type: string}, child: {$ref: Slot}}}` (child OPTIONAL) → the generated slot has `name` and NO `child` (the optional loop-closer is DROPPED — "terminates with the properties its schema REQUIRES and nothing else"); a REQUIRED loop-closer (`child` in `required`) → the key APPEARS with the empty shape (an object → `{}`, the "empty shape of the type it declares"); (5) the array-element termination matrix — a component whose element type is the recursing type: `Sections: {type: array, items: {$ref: Section}}` where Section is the recursive type — CASE A: the element requires NOTHING (`Section: {type: object, properties: {sub: {$ref: Section}}}` (no required) → the terminated array is FILLED to `minItems` with the empty shape (a `minItems: 1` section keeps its one empty element (the connect `EvaluationFormItem` exhibit — the probe: a minItems-1 array → length 1, the element the empty shape); | W519 | L439–449 "### A Swagger 2.0 body is a parameter, and it is validated" → "2.0 has no `requestBody`: the body of an operation is a parameter with `in: body`, and the conversion used to drop that parameter — so `--validate` on a 2.0 document had no body to validate at all. Now it answers `422` carrying `missing required property 'name'` and `expected integer, got string`, while a valid body still gets its `201` and its synthesised response." + "That is **1,528 specs and 10,870 operations** of the APIs-guru corpus — a third of it, every one Swagger 2.0: 10,083 declare the parameter on the operation, 775 reach it through `$ref: #/parameters/…`, 12 declare it once on the path item. The media type comes from `consumes`, operation-level first and JSON preferred; a declared non-JSON type is carried as declared, so a form body stays a form body and is still not validated (the governing rule above)." + the GET/HEAD paragraph ("9 corpus specs declare a body there, 12 of them `required` (azure botservice/mariadb/mysql, hetras, n-auth, ticketmaster, illumidesk). | W557 | L87–109 "### Sizing `parallel` — Little's law" → "The pool you need is `rate × latency`: at 100/s against a ~150ms endpoint, about 15 requests are in flight at any instant, so `parallel 20` gives headroom; `parallel 8` guarantees drops. Start there, and let the drop report tell you if you guessed low — a nonzero `dropped` count with a healthy server means the *pool* is the bottleneck, not the service." + "The generator itself doesn't cap the rate: each wakeup emits **every due slot** as a batch, so tick emission sustains 5000/s and beyond (verified in crust's own suite). Your real ceiling is downstream — the `parallel` pool × service time. A due slot is dropped only when consumer backpressure has let it go stale beyond `maxLagMs` (default 1s; TS API `load(phases, {maxLagMs})`) or the phase clock ran out — catch-up of due slots is the schedule, never a burst beyond it. Still single-process CI smoke-load and soak tooling, not a distributed load rig:" + `load 5s 400/s \| parallel 100 \| GET :3000/health --timeout 2s \| expect 200 \| stats` + pinned `{"count":2000,"wallMs":4999,"rps":400,"status":{"200":2000},"p50":0.2,"p95":0.6,"p99":1.2,"meanMs":0.2}` + "400/s offered, 400/s measured — the pool, not the pacing, is what you size." | ? | W558 | L111–132 "### Guarding against a hung upstream — `--timeout`" → "A load run has one failure mode worse than drops: an upstream that stops answering but keeps the sockets open. Without a bound, every hung request pins a `parallel` worker forever — the pool wedges, every later slot goes stale, and the run stalls instead of finishing. `--timeout <dur>` (durations `ms`/`s`/`m`) bounds **every request the stage makes**:" + `load 2s 20/s \| parallel 10 \| GET :3000/export --timeout 500ms \| stats` + pinned `{"count":40,"wallMs":2457,"rps":16,"status":{"0":40},"p50":500.8,"p95":502.8,"p99":503.5,"meanMs":501}` + "Against an endpoint that never responds, the run still *completes*: each worker cycles every 500ms, and every timed-out request yields a `{status: 0, timedOut: true, ms, url}` record — it lands in the stats histogram under `"0"`, fails `expect 200`, and the `timedOut` flag distinguishes it from a connection refusal (also `status: 0`, no flag). This is a **per-request** bound on http stages — not to be confused with `test-fixture`/`test-pipes` `--timeout` (integer ms, per fixture/line). Typo'd `--flags` on http stages are loud errors, so a misspelled `--timeuot` can't silently run unbounded." | ? | W565 | L274–295 "## Reading the report" → "The report format is picked from `--out`'s extension: `.json`, `.md`, anything else is plain text. With no `--out`, you get a colored, folder-grouped summary on stdout." + pinned report JSON `{name, iterations:1000, threads:32, stress:{mean:12.4, min:4, max:187, p50:9, p95:31, p99:62}, statusCodes:{"201":994,"409":6}}` + "Exit codes: `0` all pass, `1` any failure or error, `2` no files matched or bad args." | ? probes (scratch fixture → scratch server): (1) extension routing — -o r.json → parses as JSON; -o r.md → markdown; -o r.xyz → plain text; no -o → colored folder-grouped stdout (four runs, capture each); (2) pinned JSON shape — twin `-n100 -j4 -o r.json`: field set name/iterations/threads/stress{mean,min,max,p50,p95,p99}/statusCodes matches pin (pin's 1000/32 numbers are a historical run; shape is what's pinned); stress present since N>1 (W561(1) boundary); (3) exit codes — all-pass → 0; rigged fail → 1; `test-fixture nomatch*.crust.ts` → 2; bad arg (e.g. -nabc) → 2 |

| W566 | L297–302 "## Runner knobs for long runs" → `--timeout <ms>` — "fail any fixture whose request runs longer. A fixture's own `input.signal` wins; `--timeout` only fills the gap. Without it, one hung socket stalls a 10k-iteration run indefinitely." / `--bail` — "stop **starting** new iterations after the first fail/error; in-flight ones finish and are reported." + callout "**Teardown isolation.** … A `teardown` must touch ONLY what its own `ctx` created — a 'global cleanup' sweep (`DELETE FROM users`) shreds every iteration still running. Full resets belong outside the runner." | ? probes: (1) --timeout — hanging scratch route, `-t500`: fixture errors with timeout, run completes; control without -t timeboxed at 8s, record the stall; (2) signal precedence — fixture with own `input.signal` ~100ms vs `-t5000` → fails at ~100ms (fixture signal wins); (3) --bail — rigged server fails request 3: server log shows scheduling stops after first fail (requests ≪ -n), in-flight finish and appear in report; (4) teardown isolation — R/design note, review-only (needs concurrent DB teardowns; cross-ref W453+ teardown family) |

| W567 | L304–309 "## Tips" → 4 bullets: Bake-out runs (`--count 1` → 100 → 1000+); Thread counts (start `--threads = vCPUs`); Coverage via `random.weighted`; AOT runner — "when installed via `install.sh`, the runner is AOT-compiled to a host-arch bytecode binary at `~/.crust/bin/crust-test-fixture` for fast cold-start. The shell builtin execs that binary if present and falls back to in-process dynamic import otherwise." | ? (1)–(3) R advice, no probes; (4) AOT — probe: `ls -la ~/.crust/bin/crust-test-fixture` + `file` (Bun AOT/bytecode?), cold-start timing vs in-process optional; REVIEW: install.sh AOT step + shell builtin exec/fallback path (W16/W96 install-machinery family) |

| W569 | L1–9 frontmatter (title "One dev tail"; description "Merge every dev process into one tagged stream — with readiness probes, dependency ordering, crash-loop-capped restarts, and CI-friendly wait.") + Goal: "run the whole dev stack with one command and read one coherent log, instead of three terminals fighting for attention — and reuse the same process supervisor for CI boot ordering." | R (page framing; the five features map to sections: object specs L22, liveness L44, crash loops L64, boot order L93, wait L140) |

| W570 | L11–20 "## The shape of it" → "`procs({name: "command", …})` spawns each command and streams `{ proc, stream, line }` for every stdout/stderr line (plus an exit marker), merged as lines arrive. Children die with the pipeline — Ctrl-C stops the lot." + `procs({web: "bun run dev:web", api: "bun api.ts", tsc: "tsc -b --watch"}) | (l => l.proc + " | " + l.line)` | ? probes (scratch commands, e.g. `sh -c 'echo a; sleep 0.3; echo b'` under two names): (1) item shape — lambda `l => JSON.stringify(Object.keys(l))` → keys {proc, stream, line} (+ exit marker item's extra fields, recorded verbatim); (2) merge-as-arrives — two procs echoing with staggered sleeps, merged log interleaves by arrival; (3) exit marker — each proc emits an exit item on completion (stream name? W573 pin shows `exit` stream with "exited with code 1"); (4) children die with pipeline — a procs feeding a finite upstream: after the pipeline ends, no orphan children (pgrep check) |

| W571 | L22–42 "## Object specs — per-proc env and auto-restart" → "A spec value can be an object instead of a bare command string: `{cmd, env?, restart?, ready?, live?, after?}`." + example (web env {PORT:"3001",LOG_FORMAT:"json"}, api env {PORT:"3002"} + restart:true, tsc bare string) + bullets: `env` — "extra environment for **that process only**, merged over the inherited environment"; `restart: true` — "respawn on unexpected exit, with backoff starting at 250ms and doubling to a 2s cap. You'll see `restarting in 250ms` on the `exit` stream between attempts. A **user kill** (Ctrl-C / SIGTERM) never respawns" | ? probes: (1) env isolation — proc a `{cmd: "sh -c 'echo A=$PORT'", env: {PORT: "3001"}}` + proc b bare → a's line has PORT=3001, b's line empty/unset (per-proc merge); inherited-env passthrough twin (a var exported in the shell visible to both); (2) restart backoff schedule — `procs({f: {cmd: "sh -c 'exit 1'", restart: {max: 5}}})` → exit-stream lines `restarting in 250ms` / `500ms` / `1000ms` / `2000ms` (doubling, 2s cap — the 5th line should still read 2000ms); (3) user kill — SIGTERM to the pipeline: no respawn (recorded; the Ctrl-C twin is interactive, note it); (4) spec-key surface — object with unknown key (e.g. `foo: 1` in a spec) → loud error or ignored? (recorded verbatim) |

| W572 | L44–62 "### Liveness — `live:` restarts the unhealthy-but-alive" → "A readiness probe converges once and stops watching; `live:` keeps watching for the wedge readiness can't see. Same target forms as `ready:` (`\":3001/health\"` / `\"port:5432\"`), long form `{url?, port?, intervalMs?, probeTimeoutMs?, failures?, graceMs?}` — defaults 5s cadence / 3 consecutive failures." + live-stream lines: `probe failed (1/3)`, `recovered after 1 failed probe(s)`, `unhealthy after 3 consecutive failed probe(s)` + "at the fatal streak a restartable proc is killed through the normal escalation and respawned (liveness re-arms after the next ready), while a non-restartable one fails the whole pipeline — CI semantics, exactly like a ready-timeout." + strike-counter note: the "healthy stretch" ends when the fatal streak *began*, so a proc that answers ready then wedges still runs out of `{max}` instead of restarting forever + example `procs({api: {cmd: "bun api.ts", ready: ":3001/health", live: {url: ":3001/health", failures: 3}, restart: {max: 5}}})` | ? probes (scratch HTTP server that flips healthy→wedged at t≈2s, `live: {url: \":9911/health\", failures: 3, intervalMs: 500}` to shorten cadence for the probe): (1) live-stream message verbatims — capture the three lines exactly (`probe failed (1/3)` etc); (2) fatal streak on a restartable proc — proc killed + respawned, liveness re-arms after next ready (observe second streak still caught); (3) non-restartable + live fatal → pipeline fails (exit code + message recorded); (4) strike-counter note — `{max: 2}` + wedge loop: gives up (the streak's start, not its end, consumes the strike — probe observes giving-up at the 2nd streak, not the 3rd); (5) defaults — long form omitted: cadence ≈5s, failures 3 (probe observes probe-failure gaps ≈5s; REVIEW: default values in src) |

| W573 | L64–91 "### Capping crash loops — `restart: {max: N}`" → "Bare `restart: true` retries forever, which is right for a dev server you're actively editing and wrong for one that can't boot at all. `restart: {max: N}` gives up after N consecutive restarts:" + the page's flaky line + pinned 12-line transcript (boom/exited 1 pairs with `restarting in 250ms` → `500ms` → `1000ms` → `giving up after 3 restart(s)`) + "The counter resets after a stretch of **more than 10s of uptime** — it guards against crash *loops*, not against a long-lived server ever crashing twice." | ? probes: (1) pinned transcript — run the page's EXACT flaky line, diff the captured 12 lines vs the pin (backoff schedule 250/500/1000 doubling, message `giving up after 3 restart(s)` verbatim, stream tags `[stdout]`/`[exit]`); (2) counter reset — proc that crashes, survives 12s, crashes, survives 12s, crashes → NO giving up after 3 total (uptime > 10s resets the consecutive counter); ~40s probe, timing-dependent (recorded as such); (3) give-up aftermath — after `giving up`, the proc stays dead and the pipeline: exits when all other procs finish? or keeps running? (recorded verbatim — the pin ends at give-up) |

| W574 | L93–120 "## Boot order — `ready:` probes and `after:` dependencies" → the race framing + example `procs({db: {cmd: "docker compose up pg", ready: "port:5432"}, api: {cmd: "bun api.ts", after: "db", ready: ":3001/health", restart: {max: 3}}, web: {cmd: "bun run dev", after: "api", env: {PORT: "3001"}}})` + "Probe forms: `\":3001/health\"` / `\"http(s)://…\"` (ready = any 2xx) or `\"port:5432\"` (ready = TCP connect succeeds). Long form `{url?, port?, timeoutMs?, intervalMs?}` — defaults 30s / 250ms. Probe progress arrives on a `ready` stream" + pinned choreography transcript (`migrate [ready] waiting for db` → `db [ready] ready after 755ms (port:5432)` → migrate runs/exits 0) | ? probes (scratch: bare-TCP listener on :5999 that starts accepting after 3s as "db"; api proc with `after: "db", ready: ":9912/health"`): (1) ordering — dependent spawns only AFTER dependency's ready (timestamp evidence: api's first line > db's `ready after` line); (2) probe forms — `:N/path` HTTP-2xx (scratch server), `http://` explicit, `port:5999` TCP (the three forms each verified ready); (3) ready-stream choreography — `waiting for X` / `ready after Nms (port:5999)` message shapes verbatim (pin's `ready after 755ms (port:5432)` format); (4) defaults — long form omitted: timeout 30s, interval 250ms (probe: a slow-boot 3s server observed with interval gaps ≈250ms; REVIEW: defaults in src); (5) pinned transcript — the migrate/db shape reproduced at probe scale (the 755ms number is a historical run; format is what's pinned) |

| W575 | L122–138 "The semantics that make this CI-usable, not just cosmetic:" → bullets: (a) "`after` accepts a name or a list; procs without a `ready:` probe count as ready once spawned. Unknown names, self-dependencies, and cycles throw **before anything spawns**." (b) "Gating is **one-shot**: a dependency restarting later never re-blocks a dependent. But a dependency that dies or gives up before ever becoming ready fails the whole pipeline with `dependency \"db\" exited before becoming ready` — no half-booted stack." (c) "On probe timeout, a restartable proc is killed and respawned (readiness is re-awaited after every restart); a non-restartable one **fails the pipeline**." (d) "Teardown is thorough: children are spawned in their own process group and the whole group is SIGTERM'd when the pipeline ends, escalating to SIGKILL after 3s — grandchildren (a dev server spawned via `sh -c`, say) don't outlive the pipeline." | ? probes: (a) `after: ["a","b"]` list form works; proc without ready (bare string spec) unblocks dependents once spawned; the three bad graphs — unknown name, self-dep, 2-cycle — each throw BEFORE spawn (no child processes exist; messages verbatim); (b) one-shot — dep restarts after dependent already running: dependent NOT re-blocked; pre-ready death — dep `exit 1` immediately → message `dependency "db" exited before becoming ready` verbatim + pipeline fails; (c) timeout — dep that never becomes ready: restartable → killed+respawned (re-await each time, observe), non-restartable → pipeline fails (exit code); (d) teardown — proc = `sh -c 'sleep 300 & exec sleep 300'`-style grandchild: after pipeline end, pgrep finds NO survivors (SIGTERM→SIGKILL-after-3s path; a SIGTERM-ignoring child verifies the 3s escalation) |

| W576 | L140–160 "## `wait` — readiness as a pipeline stage" → "Sometimes the process is started elsewhere (docker compose, a CI service container) and you just need to *block until it answers*. `wait` replaces the curl-sleep retry loop:" + pinned `wait :3001/api/health --timeout 40s --interval 2s` → `{"target":":3001/api/health","ready":true,"ms":7,"attempts":1}` and `wait port:5432 --timeout 30s` → `{"target":"port:5432","ready":true,"ms":2,"attempts":1}` + "Same probe grammar as `ready:` — any 2xx for URLs, TCP connect for `port:N` — with durations like `300ms`/`30s`/`2m` (defaults 30s / 500ms). Not ready in time → `crust: wait: :3001/api/health not ready after 40s`, exit 1." + CI script block starts (L159–160: `crust -c 'wait :3000/health --timeout 40s --interval 2s'`, continues window 2) | ? probes: (1) ready item shape — fast scratch server: item keys exactly {target, ready, ms, attempts} (pin's ms 7/attempts 1 are historical; shape pinned); (2) retry cadence — boot-after-3s server, `--timeout 20s --interval 2s` → attempts ≈ 3–4 (2s gaps; ms ≈ 3000+); (3) port form — bare-TCP listener on :5555 → `{"target":"port:5555","ready":true,…}`; (4) not-ready — dead port `wait :3999/nope --timeout 2s` → message `crust: wait: :3999/nope not ready after 2s` verbatim + exit 1; (5) duration units — 300ms/30s/2m all parse (2m only syntax-checked via --check; REVIEW: default 30s/500ms in src) |

The `logs` builtin holds the process group open and buffers its recent output; every line typed at its prompt is a pipeline fragment run over that buffer, then live:" + `logs procs({web: "bun run dev", api: "bun api.ts"})` + pinned transcript (hint line `logs: items are {proc, stream, line} objects — try (l => l.line) or filter (l => l.proc === "web")`, `logs>` prompt, buffer hit `[web] ✓ Ready in 158ms` "from the buffer, even if it scrolled by minutes ago", `-- live --` separator, ^C, second fragment `filter (l => l.proc === "api") | (l => l.line)` → "only api's lines now, same stack still running") | ? probes (drive the session via stdin: `printf '(l => l.proc)\nexit\n' | crust -c 'logs procs({a: "sh -c \"echo x; sleep 0.5; echo y\"})'` — if non-TTY prompt is unsupported, record that and REVIEW the session code): (1) boot hint line verbatim (the L180 hint + the W344/W446 boot line `logs: <cmd> — buffering last 10000 items` — capture BOTH); (2) fragment-over-buffer — a proc that prints a tagged line early, session opened later, fragment filters it from the buffer (the "scrolled by minutes ago" claim at probe scale); (3) `-- live --` separator appears after buffer replay, then live lines continue; (4) exit tears the group down (pgrep clean). Buffer size: REVIEW the ring buffer (W344/W446 "last 10000 items") |

| T55 | Session environment — assignments + the two refused shapes (site L119–145 W246–W248 ↔ SKILL L114–140 SK20/SK21) | C (near-verbatim throughout: "a line of pure assignments *is* the session environment — it runs the same code `export` runs, so the two spellings cannot disagree"; the 4-line `crust` block identical (`FOO=bar`; `PATH=$PATH:/tmp/bin`; `CFLAGS+=$EXTRA_FLAGS`; `TOKEN='$literal'`); the history clause identical (`FOO=bar` used to be `sh -c 'FOO=bar'` — exit **0**, value gone, next line's `$FOO` empty); the quoting rules identical (`'…'` protects `$`, `"…"` expands, a backslash protects exactly one character, `FOO=C:\temp` survives). The two-shapes list is identical: `FOO=bar cmd args` = sh's *transient* prefix (line goes to sh untouched); `FOO=$(cmd)` **refused** rather than run (value would have to cross back out of a child; honest route = pipeline into a `capture` stage of that name; quoting doesn't change it; neither does a pipe inside the substitution — `FOO=$(date | head -1)` refused too; a `$()` anywhere else stays sh's, `echo "$(date)"` still prints the date). No contradiction.) |
| T56 | These are not shell commands — head + the base64 shadow (site L147–153 W249 ↔ SKILL L142–155 SK22) | C (same opening: "`base64`, `sort`, `head`, `wc` and friends exist as **real binaries** too. crust runs its own when the name is registered (`base64 hello` encodes the *text*, not a file), and sh's when it is not (`… | sort` is `/usr/bin/sort`)." SKILL L150–155 ADDS the base64-shadow paragraph the site's window-1 does not carry: "`/usr/bin/base64 in.ts` encodes the FILE, crust's encodes the five characters of the path (`aW4udHM=`) and now says so on stderr. A file's bytes are `base64 --file logo.png` — crust's only binary-safe route, because a pipeline decodes UTF-8 on the way (`read logo.png | base64` gives `77+9…` for a PNG whose real answer is `iVBORw0…`) — and `--out <path>` is how the answer reaches a file, since a function stage cannot be redirected." Carried: SK22 (SK side) + W252 (site Traps bullet 1 — "`base64 in.ts` encodes the path text, not the file" — the site DOES carry the shadow claim in its Traps section, window 2). The "neither takes a redirect — two different messages, because a function and a tool fail differently" lead-in is shared; the refusal blocks themselves are the next row (window 2). No contradiction.) |

## (b) Probes run

### 2026-10-18, fresh build `bin/crust-bin` (07:19)

**R24 — does a bare `FOO=x` line persist?**
```
$ CRUST_CONFIG=/dev/null ./bin/crust-bin -c $'FOO=bar\necho $FOO'
bar
exit=0
```
→ persists. X confirmed (README clause stale, pre-G04). Fixed in place `0b15108`.

**W153 — `range(0,3)` count + the `wrap` examples (page preconditions: its init.ts registers `wrap` via `crust.fn`)**
```
$ CRUST_CONFIG=/dev/null ./bin/crust-bin -c 'range(0, 3) | wc -l'
4
exit=0
$ cat /tmp/opencode/crust-w153/init.ts
crust.fn("wrap", (item: unknown, l: string, r: string) => `${l}${item}${r}`);
$ CRUST_CONFIG=/tmp/opencode/crust-w153/init.ts ./bin/crust-bin -c 'echo hi | wrap [ ]'
[hi]
exit=0
$ CRUST_CONFIG=/tmp/opencode/crust-w153/init.ts ./bin/crust-bin -c 'range(0, 3) | wrap "<" ">"'
<0>
<1>
<2>
<3>
exit=0
```
→ V: 4 items (range inclusive ✓), bare `[ ]` survives the lexer (W152's sharper case), both lines behave as documented. The first run's `wrap: command not found` (127) was a probe artifact — `CRUST_CONFIG=/dev/null` loads no init.ts, so `wrap` was never registered.

**W600 — the 4-line pre-deploy gate, as written (isolation twin: :4000→:4123, :4000 held by a local service)**
```
$ crust -c 'mock-server ./openapi.yaml -p4123' &     # mock-server: 1 route(s) … listening on http://0.0.0.0:4123
$ crust -c 'wait :4123/pets --timeout 20s'
{"target":":4123/pets","ready":true,"ms":1,"attempts":1}
wait exit=0
$ crust -c 'test-fixture fixtures/w600-4123.crust.ts'
1 file(s), 1 run(s): 1 pass, 0 fail, 0 error
test-fixture exit=0
$ crust -c 'verify-web-links http://localhost:3000 --fixtures site/*.meta.crust.ts'
verify-web-links: no sitemap entries found via robots.txt, /sitemap.xml or /sitemap-index.xml on http://localhost:3000
verify-web-links exit=2
```
→ steps 1–3 pass, step 4 can never pass: nothing in the gate starts a site on :3000. X confirmed → G16. (The mock's own refusal on the occupied :4000 — `Is port 4000 in use?` — is correct behavior, recorded for the rig notes.)
